 While it can be argued that the isoprene-responsive transcriptome may not represent the proteome accurately, the fact that isoprene emitting Arabidopsis showed phenotypes similar to those seen in *phyB* mutants and isoprene emitting tobacco showed phenotypes similar to JA signaling indicates that the gene expression patterns seen in the transcriptome is also represented in the phenome of isoprene emitting lines. Isoprene mediated enhancement of CK biosynthesis and growth in Arabidopsis (K.G.S. Dani, S. Pollastri, M.Reichelt, T.D. Sharkey, and F. Loreto, unpublished) show that isoprene-mediated effects on gene expression do in fact translate to isoprene mediated changes in the proteome. As mentioned before, GA, CK, and ABA are all synthesized by prenyl phosphates from the MEP pathway and it is possible that isoprene, through its effects on gene expression, is altering flux through the MEP pathway in favor of hormone biosynthesis. Additional evidence showing that alterations of the isoprene responsive transcriptome leads to representative changes in the metabolome was provided by Behnke et al. (2010), and was discussed in detail in the previous sections. In addition, the upregulated and downregulated genes of a certain growth regulator pathway corresponded to a similar trend,

either towards an overall enhancement or overall suppression of that particular pathway. These
data provide clear evidence that isoprene can act as a signaling molecule. In addition, the ability
of isoprene to target genes encoding for membrane proteins and cell surface receptors,
transporters, proteins that can trigger signaling cascades, key proteins in secondary metabolite
and growth regulator biosynthesis, and variety of transcription factors solidifies the potential role

1007 of isoprene as a signaling molecule.

1008 Given the fact that ISPS has a higher temperature optimum and isoprene synthesis increases with 1009 increasing temperature (and light), isoprene as a signaling molecule may be especially effective 1010 under high temperature, and high light stress. If isoprene is a signaling molecule, why evolution favored a volatile hydrocarbon as a signaling molecule and why it is produced in such large 1011 quantities can only be speculated. For example, the intercellular ethylene concentrations is about 1012 0.5  $\mu$ L L<sup>-1</sup> (Aharoni and Lieberman, 1979); for isoprene it is about 20  $\mu$ L L<sup>-1</sup> (Singsaas et al., 1013 1014 1997). It is possible that isoprene, while protecting plants under stress, also acts as a plant-to-1015 plant long distance signal to prime neighboring plants for impending stress, or act as an attractant 1016 to pollinators, or predators, or a repellor of other herbovores or pathogens. Perhaps this is why 1017 isoprene is made in such large amounts. However, this hypothesis can be argued against because 1018 once airborne, isoprene is short lived and gets diluted to minute amounts (Barker et 1., 2005). 1019 While it is possible that insects can detect minute amounts of volatile chemicals in the air, and 1020 that isoprene concentrations within a micro-environment of a plant population may increase to high enough levels to be sufficient to induce signaling in neighboring plants, these hypothesis 1021 remain to be tested. 1022

1023

# 1024 CONCLUSIONS

1025 The present study was designed to understand how isoprene affects growth and physiology in 1026 unstressed plants, whether isoprene can act as a signaling molecule and to shed light on the 1027 potential mechanisms through which isoprene influences stress responses in plants. Although 1028 Arabidopsis (and tobacco) do not normally produce isoprene, when engineered to do so, the 1029 same responses to temperature,  $CO_2$  and light can be observed as in native emitters. Regardless 1030 of the species, isoprene had a positive effect on leaf pigment production. Isoprene also had a 1031 significant positive effect on seedling growth, and both vegetative and reproductive growth in  Arabidopsis under unstressed conditions. Transcriptomic data revealed that isoprene probably exerts its effects on growth and stress tolerance by affecting gene expression of many growth regulators and molecular networks involved in abiotic and biotic stress tolerance. Upregulation of growth and defense related signaling pathways simultaneously may lead to plants that grow better and are also protected against stress. In some cases, growth-defense tradeoffs may be one reason why isoprene mediated growth phenotypes are variable between species. Our data show that isoprene is beneficial for plants not only under stress conditions, but, also under unstressed conditions and introduces a novel role of isoprene as a signaling molecule. Some isoprene- responsive signaling networks revealed during the present study were affiliated with abiotic stresses (e.g., soil acidity, salt stress, heavy metal toxicity) and biotic stresses (e.g., necrotrophic pathogens) that were not known to be protected against by isoprene in plants. The ability of isoprene to protect plants against these stresses should be further explored.

#### **MATERIALS AND METHODS**

**Plant Materials**

#### *Arabidopsis thaliana*

 For the transgenic Arabidopsis, the complete cDNA sequence for *ISPS* from *E. globulus* including the sequence encoding the native transit peptide was placed behind an Arabidopsis rubisco small subunit promoter *rbcS-1A* (At1g67090). The first 1,000 bp of the promoter sequence starting from the adenine immediately preceding the ATG start codon and counting backward was used. It has been shown by deletion analysis that this region promotes a high transcript amount (Donald and Cashmore, 1990). An octopine synthase terminator was used following the isoprene synthase gene. DNA for the *E. globulous ISPS* and the *rbcS-1A* promoter and octopine synthase terminator were synthesized by Bio Basic Inc. (Markham Ontario, Canada) and placed in the pUC57 plasmid vector. The construct was then transferred to the pART27 destination vector (Gleave, 1992). The construct was also transferred to the pART27 vector without the *ISPS* to generate the empty vector control. The pART27 vector constructs were transformed into *Agrobacterium* strain GV3101 by electroporation. Arabidopsis Col-0 was transformed using the floral dip method (Clough and Bent, 1998). Transformation of Arabidopsis 1061 was carried out at the Arabidopsis Transformation Facility for the Plant Research Laboratory,1062 Michigan State University.

1063 Seeds from primary transformants were selected on plates containing 0.8% Phytoblend (Caisson

Laboratories, North Logan UT), 4.3 g•L<sup>-1</sup> MS salts (MSP C0130, Caisson Laboratories), 1%

sucrose, 2.5 mM MES, and 50  $\mu$ g•mL<sup>-1</sup> kanamycin using a rapid selection method to generate T<sub>1</sub>

1066 plants (Harrison et al., 2006). T<sub>1</sub> plants were grown in Redi-Earth growing media (Michigan

1067 Grower Products, MI, USA) and allowed to self-pollinate. Approximately 100 seeds from T<sub>1</sub>

1068 plants were plated on kanamycin selection plates and those with a 3:1 ratio of resistant plants to

susceptible plants were selected and grown to maturity on Redi-Earth (T<sub>2</sub> generation).

1070 Approximately 100 seeds from each plant selected in the T<sub>2</sub> generation were planted and those

1071 plants with 100% resistance were selected and used for all further experiments (T<sub>3</sub> generation).

1072 Transgene insertion was confirmed by PCR.

1073 Three empty vector lines (EV-A2, EV-B3, and EV-F1) and seven *ISPS*-containing lines (A1, B2,

1074 B4, C1, C3, C4, and F2) of Arabidopsis representing independent transformation events were

1075 characterized during this study in terms of isoprene emission, photosynthesis, and growth. For

1076 this purpose T3 plants along with the wild-type (Col-0) were grown on Suremix growing

1077 medium (Michigan Grower Products, MI, USA) under a 12 h photoperiod, 120 μmol•m<sup>-2</sup>•s<sup>-1</sup> light

1078 intensity, 23°C day-time and 20°C night-time temperatures, and 60% relative humidity. Plants

1079 were fertilized every 3 days using 1/2 strength Hoagland's nutrient solution.

1080 After characterization of the seven isoprene-emitting lines, one of the empty vector lines (EV-

1081 B3) and the two lines that exhibited the highest rates of isoprene emission (B2 and C4) were

selected and grown simultaneously with the tobacco lines to compare the effects of *ISPS* 

1083 expression on isoprene emission and photosynthesis, the response of isoprene emission and

1084 photosynthesis to temperature, CO<sub>2</sub>, light and darkness, the post-illumination isoprene emission,

1085 MEP pathway metabolites, and growth, between the two species. To test whether the observed

1086 effects of isoprene on the above parameters in Arabidopsis were not due to a light effect, EV-B3,

1087 B2, and C4 were grown on Redi-Earth growing media and kept in either a low light growth

1088 chamber (100  $\mu$ mol•m<sup>-2</sup>•s<sup>-1</sup>) or a high light growth chamber (200  $\mu$ mol•m<sup>-2</sup>•s<sup>-1</sup>).

1089 *Tobacco* 

1090 Seeds of *Nicotiana tabacum* cy. Samsun transformed with the isoprene synthase gene (*ISPS*) 1091 from P. alba to produce isoprene-emitting homozygous plants (IE) and the corresponding non-1092 emitting azygous (null segregants) control (NE) were obtained from Claudia Vickers, University of Queensland. The NE and IE lines used in the present study correspond to line 32 published in 1093 1094 Vickers et al. (2009b) where IE was reported to produce the highest amount of isoprene compared to the other isoprene emitting tobacco lines. More information regarding plasmid 1095 1096 construction, transformation of tobacco, and line selection is described in Vickers et al. (2009b). These seeds were used to grow plants in a growth chamber (Big-Foot, BioChambers, under a 16 1097 h photoperiod at a light intensity of 400  $\mu$ mol·m<sup>-2</sup>·s<sup>-1</sup>, day/night temperature of 25°C/22°C, and 1098 humidity of 60%) to collect seeds. For experiments reported here, plants were grown from these 1099 seeds in Suremix growing medium (Michigan Grower Products, MI, USA) and kept in Michigan 1100 State University greenhouses. Plants were fertilized every 2-3 days using 1/2 strength 1101

- 1102 Hoagland's nutrient solution (Hoagland and Amon, 1938).
- 1103

# 1104 Transgene Detection in Arabidopsis Transformed with *ISPS*

#### 1105 **DNA Extraction**

1106 DNA was extracted from 7-week old rosette leaves using a DNeasy Plant Mini Kit (Qiagen

1107 Hilden, Germany) based on manufacturer's instructions. The DNA concentration and purity were

1108 determined with the aid of a DeNovix DS-11 spectrophotometer (DeNovix, Wilmington, DE).

## 1109 Polymerase Chain Reaction (PCR)

1110 PCR was carried out using 1  $\mu$ L of the extracted DNA as the template, in a total volume of 50  $\mu$ L

that contained 1X PCR buffer, 2.5 mM MgCl<sub>2</sub>, 150 μM deoxyribonucleoside triphosphate mix, 1

1112 unit of Taq DNA polymerase (all reagents supplied by Invitrogen, Carlsbad, CA), and 1 µM each

- 1113 of the forward and reverse primers Primer sequences were TGTGGAATTGTGAGCGGATA
- 1114 (forward), GTGTCTCTAAGGGTGCGGTC (reverse). PCR was carried out based on the
- 1115 manufacturer's instructions in an Eppendorf Mastercycler® RealPlex<sup>2</sup> PCR machine (Eppendorf,
- 1116 Hamburg, Germany). A PCR mix without the template DNA was used as a negative control. A
- 1117 PCR mix with empty vector plasmid was used as a positive control. After PCR, 10 µL of the
- 1118 resulting amplicons were each mixed with 2.5 µL of EZ-vision loading buffer/DNA dye

- 1119 (Amresco, Cleveland, OH), and run on a 1% agarose gel (0.5g agarose, 50 mL 1X Tris-acetate-
- 1120 EDTA buffer TAE) at 85 V for 30 min. A 1 Kb Plus DNA ladder (Invitrogen, Carlsbad, CA)
- 1121 was run alongside the amplicons. The separated amplicons were visualized under UV light.
- 1122

# 1123 Gas Exchange Measurements

1124 The experimental set-up for gas exchange was as described by Li et al. (2011). A custom-built

- rosette gas exchange cuvette connected to a LI-6400 was used for Arabidopsis. Tobacco leaves
- 1126 were enclosed in the LED leaf chamber (6 cm<sup>2</sup>) of LI-6400 Portable Photosynthesis System (LI-
- 1127 COR Biosciences, Lincoln, NE, USA). The exhaust gas of LI-6400 was connected to a Fast
- 1128 Isoprene Sensor (FIS, Hills Scientific, Boulder, CO, USA) where isoprene emission was
- averaged over 5-s intervals. Experiments were carried out under standard conditions (30°C, 400
- 1130  $\mu$ mol•mol<sup>-1</sup>CO<sub>2</sub> concentration, 800  $\mu$ mol•m<sup>-2</sup>•s<sup>-1</sup> photosynthetic photon flux density at leaf level
- 1131 for tobacco and 500  $\mu$ mol·m<sup>-2</sup>·s<sup>-1</sup> for Arabidopsis). The temperature of the leaf chamber was
- 1132 controlled by a water bath. Light for Arabidopsis was provided with a SL3500 white light
- emitting diode array (Photon Systems Instruments, Covina Cal. USA).
- 1134 To determine temperature response of photosynthesis and isoprene production, the temperature
- 1135 of the leaf chamber was controlled by two water baths linked by a six-way valve that allowed
- 1136 ultrafast switching between different temperatures and simultaneous measurements of
- 1137 photosynthesis and isoprene emissions were recorded at each temperature point: at 23, 30, 35, 40
- 1138 and  $45^{\circ}$ C for Arabidopsis, and at 25, 30, 35 and  $40^{\circ}$ C for tobacco.
- 1139 For  $CO_2$  response, the reference  $CO_2$  concentration in the leaf chamber was set at 0, 62.5, 125,
- 1140 250, 500, and 1,000  $\mu$ mol·mol<sup>-1</sup>. When the set CO<sub>2</sub> concentration was achieved, a 4-min
- stabilization period was allowed prior to recording photosynthesis and isoprene emission.
- 1142 For the light response, photosynthesis and isoprene emissions were recorded after 4-min
- stabilization period under each light level. For Arabidopsis, it was set at 0, 100, 200, 500, 1000,
- and 1,500  $\mu$ mol·m<sup>-2</sup>·s<sup>-1</sup>. For tobacco, the light intensity was set at 0, 200, 500, 1,000, and 2,000
- 1145  $\mu$ mol•m<sup>-2</sup>•s<sup>-1</sup>.

#### **Measurement of Post-Illumination Isoprene Emission**

 Post-illumination isoprene emission was measured using the procedures described in Li et al. (2011). Light in the leaf chamber was turned off by either covering the Arabidopsis rosette gas exchange cuvette with black cloth or by instantly switching off the LI-6400 light source in the case of tobacco. The post-illumination isoprene emission was measured for 30 min. The chamber clearing time was determined by injecting isoprene directly into the chamber and then abruptly stopping the injection to follow how quickly isoprene cleared from the chamber and tubing.

#### **Determination of MEP Pathway Metabolites**

 Whole rosettes of Arabidopsis and whole leaves from tobacco were harvested into liquid N2. The tissue was then ground in liquid N<sup>2</sup> and cell contents were extracted with a solvent containing acetonitrile: water (4:1). The extract was centrifuged at 14,000 g for 10 min and the supernatant was stored at -80°C for later analysis. The MEP pathway metabolites were separated and quantified by Acquity TQD Tandem Quadrupole Mass Spectrometer with Acquity BEH Amide column (Acquity Group, Chicago, USA). Standards of MEP pathway metabolites were separated using a binary gradient consisting of 50 mM ammonium acetate in acetonitrile adjusted to pH 10.0 with ammonium hydroxide. The measurement procedure was described in detail by Li and Sharkey (2013a). All metabolites were measured using mass spectrometry as described above except DMADP which was calculated using the post-illumination isoprene response (Rasulov et al. 2009; Weise et al. 2013).

#### **Plant Growth Analyses**

#### *Comparison of Young Seedling Growth Characteristics*

- Cotyledons of Arabidopsis and tobacco seedlings were photographed and heights of the
- hypocotyls were measured at different time points up to 12 days after seed sowing. Photographs
- were used to measure cotyledon area using Image J software (National Institutes of Health,
- Bethesda, MD, USA).
- *Determination of Leaf Area, Curling, Dry Weight, Growth Rate and Inflorescence Growth*

 Every week, intact Arabidopsis rosettes were photographed from the top to obtain projected leaf area. After gas exchange measurements, leaves were harvested from each rosette and were placed on a bench top and photographed to obtain apparent total leaf area. Next, the same leaves were flattened by covering with transparent Plexiglas and photographed again to obtain actual total leaf area. The Image J software was used to measure leaf area from the photographs. Leaf curling in Arabidopsis was calculated as: (actual total leaf area – apparent total leaf area)/ actual total leaf area×100%. Tobacco leaves were also photographed to determine leaf area. The leaf number was noted in Arabidopsis rosettes. After photographing, leaves and stems from Arabidopsis and tobacco were freeze-dried (Labconco freeze-dryer, Labconco Corporation, Kansas, MO, USA) followed by dry weight measurements. Leaf mass per unit leaf area (LMA) was calculated as the ratio between leaf dry weight and total leaf area. The number of days required for inflorescence initiation and height of the inflorescence stem over time was measured in Arabidopsis. Absolute growth rates: leaf growth (m<sup>2</sup> per day) or stem growth (m per day), were calculated using projected leaf area and stem heights obtained at different time points of the life cycle in Arabidopsis.

#### **Measurement of Photosynthetic Pigment Content**

 Two leaf discs were harvested with the aid of a cork borer (diameter of 8.75 mm) from two mature leaves. Leaf discs were ground in liquid N<sup>2</sup> using a mortar and pestle, and the photosynthetic pigments were extracted with 2 ml of 96% ethanol. The content of chlorophyll (Chl) a, Chl b and carotenoids were calculated using the equations described by Lichtenthaler and Wellburn (1983), and the pigment concentration was calculated using the leaf disc area. As shading can affect pigment content, tobacco plants were maintained under uniform light, and kept sufficiently apart to avoid shading.

#### **Analysis of Gene Expression**

# *RNA Extraction*

Out of the seven isoprene-emitting Arabidopsis lines, the empty vector line EV-B3 and B2, the

line that exhibited the highest isoprene emission rate, were selected for gene expression

profiling; for tobacco NE and IE (line 32) lines were used. Leaves were harvested from 5½-week

- old Arabidopsis and 7-week old tobacco plants grown under normal growth conditions
- (unstressed) as described above. Arabidopsis plants grown under 200 μmol•m-2 •s-1 were used.
- Leaves from three plants were harvested from each line (three independent RNA samples per
- line). Total RNA was extracted using the RNeasy Mini Kit (QIAGEN, Hilden, Germany). The
- RNA concentration, integrity, and quality were determined with the aid of a Qubit RNA Broad-
- Range Assay Kit (Invitrogen, Carlsbad, CA) and a Qubit 4 benchtop fluorometer (Invitrogen,
- Carlsbad, CA). RNA integrity was further assessed with a 2100 Bioanalyzer (Agilent
- Technologies, Santa Clara, CA). All samples used for sequencing had a RNA integrity number of
- at least 7.0 (1 being the most degraded RNA profile and 10 being the most intact).

# *mRNA Sequencing (RNA-Seq)*

- Single-end 50-bp mRNA sequencing was performed at the Michigan State University Research
- Technology Support Facility Genomics Core (https://rtsf.natsci.msu.edu/genomics/). Libraries
- were prepared using the Illumina TruSeq Stranded mRNA Library Preparation Kit (Illumina, San
- Diego, CA) following the manufacturer's recommendations. Completed libraries were assessed
- for quality and quantified using a combination of *Qubit dsDNA* High Sensitivity Assay
- Kit (Qubit, Carlsbad, CA), Caliper LabChipGX System and a DNA High Sensitivity Assay Kit
- (Caliper Life Sciences, Waltham, MA), and Kapa Illumina Library Quantification qPCR assays
- (Illumina, San Diego, CA). Each set of libraries was pooled in equimolar quantities and loaded
- on one lane of an Illumina HiSeq 2500 High Output flow cell (v4) (Illumina, San Diego, CA).
- Sequencing was carried out in a 1x50 bp (SE50) format using HiSeq SBS reagents (v4)
- (Illumina, San Diego, CA). Base calling was done by Illumina Real Time Analysis (RTA)
- v1.18.64 and output of RTA was demultiplexed and converted to FastQ format with Illumina
- Bcl2fastq v1.8.4. The average number of sequencing reads was 44.7±4.3 million per sample for
- Arabidopsis and 46.7±2.8 million per sample for tobacco.

# *Data Analysis*

- RNA-seq next-generation sequencing data were subjected to gene differential expression (DE)
- analysis. Briefly, sequencing adapters and low quality bases were trimmed from sequencing
- reads using Trimmomatic version 0.32 (Bolger et al., 2014), and cleaned reads were examined
- with FASTQC [\(https://www.bioinformatics.babraham.ac.uk/projects/fastqc/\)](https://www.bioinformatics.babraham.ac.uk/projects/fastqc/) for quality

 evaluation. Reads were aligned to the reference genomes, *A. thaliana* TAIR10 (Berardini et al., 2015) and *N. tabacum* TN90 (Sierro et al., 2014). To calculate read counts for each gene, Rsubread version 3 (Liao et al., 2013) tool (featureCounts function) was used to build read count matrix. Read count matrix was analyzed using the linear-model based Limma::Voom package (Law et al., 2014) to calculate the gene expression values (RPKM) and perform gene DE statistical analyses. Data presented in **Supplementary Files 1 and 2** represent the gene expression values RPKM units per gene. Statistical data analyses were adjusted for multiple comparisons and adjusted P values are provided at a false discovery rate (FDR) threshold of 0.05. Differentially expressed genes identified in Arabidopsis and tobacco expressing *ISPS* from the present study and Arabidopsis fumigated with isoprene (Harvey and Sharkey, 2016) were compared to identify differentially expressed genes common to at least two or all three systems; these genes were considered as isoprene responsive genes.

#### **Calculations and Statistical Analyses**

Growth analyses, measurement of photosynthesis, isoprene emission, and leaf pigment

concentrations were repeated to confirm repeatability of results. Unless stated otherwise, data

were collected from five or more biological replicates from each Arabidopsis and tobacco line.

Statistical analyses were performed using SPSS 25 (IBM, Armonk, NY). One-way ANOVA were

carried out followed by a Fisher's Least Significant Difference Test to compare the differences

between means. Graphs were drawn with Origin 8.0.

# **Accession Numbers**

The raw and processed data were deposited in the Gene Expression Omnibus (Edgar et al., 2002)

and are accessible through GEO Series accession number GSE121676 [\(https://www.ncbi.](https://www.ncbi/)nlm.

nih.gov/geo/query/acc.cgi?acc=GSE121676)

Accession numbers: *E. globulus ISPS* used to transform *Arabidopsis* **-** AB266390. *P. alba ISPS*

used to transform tobacco - EF638224 (Vickers et al. 2009b).

**Figure legends**

**Figure 1. Carbon assimilation and isoprene emission in Arabidopsis expressing** *ISPS***.** Photosynthesis (A) and isoprene emission (B) were measured simultaneously in Arabidopsis wild-type Col-0, empty vector controls (EV-A2, EV-B3, EV-F1), and lines expressing *ISPS* (C3, B4, A1, F2, C1, C4, and B2). Measurements were made on plants 45 to 47 days old. All transgenic lines belonged to the T3 generation. Values in graphs represent mean ± SE, n = 5-7 plants per line. Arabidopsis lines expressing *ISPS* which are significantly different from Col-0 and empty vector lines EV-A2 and EV-B3 are marked with asterisks. \*\**P*<0.01 using one-way ANOVA followed by a Fisher's Least Significant Difference Test.

**Figure 2. Figure 12. Proposed model for how isoprene signaling can affect GA-mediated growth 1444 1445 regulation and JA-mediated defense responses. Transcriptomic data revealed that isoprene can alter genes required for both GA accumulation and JA synthesis (genes shown in green font), 1446 which can promote both GA-mediated growth and JA-mediated defense simultaneously. We 1447 speculate that the observed growth enhancement in Arabidopsis engineered to emit isoprene is 1448 1449 likely a result of upregulation of PIF. However, interactions between GA and JA pathways occur through DELLA and JAZ proteins (Campos et al., 2016). JA synthesis leads to the degradation of 1450 1451 JAZ proteins that release the inhibition of transcription factors to enhance defense-related processes (Campos et al., 2016). Antagonistic interactions between JAZ and DELLA proteins 1452

 play a part in regulating the growth-defense trade-off mediated by GA and JA (Campos et al. 2016). Therefore, one possible explanation for the observed variations in isoprene-mediated growth effects in different species is the likely effect of isoprene on growth-defense trade-off. Genes belonging to other signalling pathways whose expression was altered by isoprene were omitted from this diagram for the sake of simplicity. Dotted lines and genes written in green font denote isoprene-responsive gene expression revealed during the present study. Asterisks denote genes differentially expressed in both Arabidopsis expressing *ISPS* and Arabidopsis fumigated by isoprene, but not differentially expressed in tobacco. Upregulation and downregulation of gene expression is denoted by pointed and blunt-ended arrows, respectively (DFL2 and TEM1 expression was downregulated in the presence of isoprene in Arabidopsis). Solid lines denote signalling pathways that are well established. Abbreviations: BBD - Bifunctional nuclease in basal defense response; CBF - CRT/DRE binding factor; COP1 - Constitutively photomorphogenic 1, a ubiquitin ligase; CRPK1- Cold-responsive protein kinase 1; DELLA - PIF transcription factor repressors; DFL2 - Dwarf in light 2, a GH3-related (auxin response related) protein; FT - Flowering locus T; GA - gibberellic acid; HY5 - Elongated hypocotyl 5; JA - jasmonic acid; JAZ - Jasmonate ZIM-domain repressors; JMT - Jasmonic acid carboxyl methyltransferase; LOX - Lipoxygenase; MARD1 - Mediator of ABA (abscisic acid)-regulated dormancy 1, a novel zinc-finger protein; MYC2 - a basic helix-loop-helix transcription factor and a master regulator of JA signaling; MYB59 - MYB domain protein 59; OPR3 - Oxophytodienoate-reductase 3; PHYB - Phytochrome-B; PIF - Phytochrome-interacting factors, TEM1 - Tempranillo 1, a RAV transcription factor; TZF5 -Tandem CCCH zinc finger protein 5; 14-3-3 - highly conserved acidic proteins of the 14-3-3-protein family.

**Figure 1. Carbon assimilation and isoprene emission in Arabidopsis expressing** *ISPS*. Photosynthesis (A) and isoprene emission (B) was measured simultaneously in Arabidopsis wild-type Col-0, empty vector controls (EV-A2, EV-B3, EV-F1), and lines expressing *ISPS* (C3, B4, A1, F2, C1, C4, and B2). Measurements were made on plants 45-to 47-days old. All transgenic lines belonged to the T3 generation. Values in graphs represent mean  $\pm$  SE, n = 5-7 plants per line. Arabidopsis lines expressing *ISPS* which are significantly different from Col-0 and empty vector lines EV-A2 and EV-B3 are marked with asterisks. \*\*P<0.01. **Figure 2. Early seedling growth in Arabidopsis expressing** *ISPS*. A comparison of hypocotyl length (A and B) and cotyledon size (C and D) in 7-day old Arabidopsis wild-type Col-0, empty vector controls (EV-A2, EV-B3, EV-F1), and lines expressing *ISPS* (C3, B4, A1, F2, C1, C4, and B2) is presented. All transgenic lines belonged to the T3 generation. Values in graphs represent mean  $\pm$  SE. In B, n = 22-41; in D, n = 12-26 plants per line. Arabidopsis lines expressing *ISPS* which are significantly different from Col-0 and empty vector lines EV-A2 and EV-B3 are marked with asterisks. +P<0.1 + P<0.05 + P<0.01. In D, C1 was only statistically different from Col-0, EV-B3 and EV-F1.

Α

Figure 3. Rosette and inflorescence growth in Arabidopsis expressing *ISPS*. Photographs comparing rosette size and projected leaf area of 21-day (A), and 50-day (B) old T3 plants, and leaf dry weight (C), shoot (leaf+inflorescence) dry weight (D), number of days required for inflorescence initiation (G), and the height of the primary (1°) inflorescence axis, in 50-day old Arabidopsis wild-type Col-0, empty vector controls (EV-A2, EV-B3, EV-F1) and lines expressing *ISPS* (C3, B4, A1, F2, C1, C4, and B2) is presented. All transgenic lines belonged to the T3 generation. Values in graphs represent mean  $\pm$  SE. In C-D, n = 4-8; in E-F, n = 3-6 plants per line. Arabidopsis lines expressing *ISPS* which are significantly different from Col-0 and empty vector lines EV-A2 and EV-B3 are marked with asterisks.  $^+P<0.1 * P<0.05 * * P<0.01$ ; in (E) and (F) asterisks denote statistical comparisons relative to only EV-A2 and EV-B3.

Figure 4. Rosette and inflorescence stem growth rates in Arabidopsis expressing *ISPS*. Projected leaf area over time (A), and absolute growth rates calculated as the increase in projected leaf area per day (B), and increase in the height of the 1° inflorescence axis (stem) per day (C) are presented. DAS - days after seeding. All transgenic lines belonged to the T3 generation. Values in graphs represent mean  $\pm$  SE. In A-B, n = 4-6 plants per line. In C, n = 3-5 plants per line. Arabidopsis lines expressing *ISPS* which are significantly different from Col-0 and empty vector lines EV-A2 and EV-B3 are marked with asterisks. +P<0.1 \*P<0.05 \*\*P<0.01.
